## 1. Rules Of Construction

**Result:** FAIL — 3.56/4.00

**Case ID:** zr-rules-of-construction

**Jurisdiction / edition:** New York City, New York; NYC Zoning Resolution — text through 2026-08-13

**Project context:** `{}`

**Question:** How does the Zoning Resolution instruct a reader to resolve a conflict between the enacted text and an illustration or summary table?

### Selected evidence

**ZR 12-01 — canonical section 20018521**

> The following rules of construction apply to the text of this Resolution:

(a) The particular shall control the general.

(b) In case of any difference of meaning or implication between the text of this Resolution and any caption, illustration, summary table or illustrative table, the text shall control.

(c) The word "shall" is always mandatory and not discretionary. The word "may" is permissive.

(d) Words used in the present tense shall include the future; and words used in the singular number shall include the plural, and the plural the singular, unless the context clearly indicates the contrary.

(e) A "building" or "structure" includes any part thereof. The terms residential building, commercial building and community facility building shall refer to an entire building used exclusively for such use.

(f) The phrase "used for" includes "arranged for", "designed for", "intended for", "maintained for", "or occupied for".

(g) The word "person" includes an individual, a corporation, a partnership, an incorporated association or any other similar entity.

(h) Unless the context clearly indicates the contrary, where a regulation involves two or more items, conditions, provisions, or events connected by the conjunction "and", "or" or "either...or", the conjunction shall be interpreted as follows:

(1) "and" indicates that all the connected items, conditions, provisions or events shall apply;

(2) "or" indicates that the connected items, conditions, provisions or events may apply singly or in any combination; and

(3) "either...or" indicates that the connected items, conditions, provisions or events shall apply singly but not in combination.

(i) The word "includes" shall not limit a term to the specified examples, but is intended to extend its meaning to all other instances or circumstances of like kind or character.

(j) References within a Section or cross-references to a Section numbered with four digits shall include all following Sections with numbers whose first four digits are identical with such Section number but references or cross-references to a Section numbered with five digits shall refer only to such specific five-digit Section. For Sections starting with 101-00, references within a Section or cross-references to a Section numbered with five digits shall include all following Sections with numbers whose first five digits are identical with such Section number but references or cross-references to a Section numbered with six digits shall refer only to such specific six-digit Section.

### Permitext answer

Across the listed Use Group I uses, M1 is generally the most permissive district: it allows every listed use either as-of-right or by special permit. M2 and M3 have identical allowances in this table, allowing agricultural uses (with P and U notations), outdoor skating rinks, and sand, gravel, or clay pits by special permit; they do not permit cemeteries, golf courses, outdoor racket courts, or public/private parks or playgrounds.

More specifically: agricultural uses are permitted in M1, M2, and M3 with additional conditions and open-use allowances (●P U); outdoor skating rinks are permitted in all three (●); and sand, gravel, or clay pits require a special permit in all three (○). Cemeteries, golf courses, outdoor racket courts, and public parks/playgrounds or private parks are permitted only in M1; the racket-court entry also carries additional conditions (●P).

The symbols mean: ● permitted; ♦ permitted with limitations; ○ special permit required; and – not permitted. A second notation can add S for a size restriction, P for additional conditions, or U for open-use allowances. The table also states that an Open Use permitted as-of-right must be unenclosed, except for ancillary buildings or other structures.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- M1, M2, and M3 allowances: The table permits agricultural uses in M1, M2, and M3 with P and U notations; permits outdoor skating rinks in all three; and requires a special permit for sand, gravel, or clay pits in all three. It permits cemeteries, golf courses, outdoor racket courts, and public parks or playgrounds or private parks only in M1, while listing those uses as not permitted in M2 and M3. Thus, for the uses shown, M2 and M3 have the same allowances and are more restrictive than M1.
- Table symbols and open-use condition: The table legend defines ● as permitted, ♦ as permitted with limitations, ○ as special permit required, – as not permitted, S as size restriction, P as additional conditions, and U as open use allowances. It further states that listed Open Uses permitted as-of-right in a Manufacturing District must be unenclosed, except for ancillary buildings or other structures.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- None

**Citations**

- ZR 42-111: Selected governing table establishing the listed Use Group I allowances in M1, M2, and M3 and defining the displayed symbols.

- [x] concept-2: The answer distinguishes M1 from M2 and M3, identifies that M2 and M3 match for the displayed rows, and accurately explains the legend symbols and S/P/U notations. (high confidence; objective).
- [x] concept-3: The answer confines its conclusions to the listed rows and expressly states that it does not establish matters, such as the content of additional conditions or special-permit standards, absent from the selected table. (high confidence; objective).

### Permitext answer

Appendix J establishes that certain designated areas within Manufacturing Districts are defined by the Appendix J maps. It divides those mapped areas into two regulatory groups for self-service storage facilities: Subarea 1 areas are subject to the as-of-right provisions of ZR 42-19, while Subarea 2 areas are subject to the City Planning Commission special-permit provision in ZR 74-192.

The selected tables identify the designated-area names, borough/community-district references, and corresponding map numbers. Subarea 1 lists Bathgate, Steinway, Richmond Hill, West Shore, and Rossville; Subarea 2 lists the additional named areas shown in the selected table, from Port Morris through North Shore.

No site-specific conclusion can be made from these excerpts alone as to whether a particular lot is inside an Appendix J designated area, which subarea applies, or whether the as-of-right or special-permit path governs. That requires the applicable Appendix J map and the site's identified location plotted against that map; neither is supplied here.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Mapped designated areas and their regulatory distinction: Appendix J states that boundaries of certain designated areas within Manufacturing Districts are shown on its maps. It assigns self-service storage facilities in mapped Subarea 1 areas to the as-of-right provisions of ZR 42-19 and those in mapped Subarea 2 areas to the City Planning Commission special-permit provisions of ZR 74-192.
- Subarea 1 table entries: The Subarea 1 table identifies Bathgate (Bronx Community Districts 3, 4, and 6; Map 1), Steinway (Queens Community District 1; Maps 1 and 2), Richmond Hill (Queens Community District 9; Map 1), West Shore (Staten Island Community Districts 1 and 2; Maps 1 through 3), and Rossville (Staten Island Community District 3; Map 1).
- Subarea 2 table entries: The Subarea 2 table identifies the named designated areas, their borough/community-district references, and their map numbers, including Port Morris, Hunts Point, Zerega, Eastchester, Brooklyn Navy Yard, Southwest Brooklyn, Flatlands/Fairfield, East New York, Ridgewood, Williamsburg/Greenpoint, North Brooklyn/Long Island City/Maspeth, Maspeth/North Brooklyn, Long Island City, Woodside, Jamaica, JFK, and North Shore.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- The subject site's identified location and its position on the applicable Appendix J map.
- The applicable Appendix J map or maps needed to determine whether the site falls within a mapped designated-area boundary.

**Citations**

- ZR APPENDIX J: Establishes that Appendix J boundaries are map-based and distinguishes Subarea 1 as-of-right treatment from Subarea 2 special-permit treatment for self-service storage facilities.
- ZR APPENDIX J: Establishes the listed Subarea 1 table entries and associated map numbers.
- ZR APPENDIX J: Establishes the listed Subarea 2 table entries and associated map numbers.

### Rubric checks

**Required concepts**

- [x] concept-1: The answer expressly identifies and cites Appendix J, including a citations section referencing “ZR APPENDIX J.” (high confidence; objective).
- [x] concept-2: The answer accurately distinguishes Subarea 1 as subject to the as-of-right provisions of ZR 42-19 and Subarea 2 as subject to the City Planning Commission special-permit provision in ZR 74-192. (high confidence; objective).
- [x] concept-3: The answer expressly states that a determination for a particular lot requires the applicable Appendix J map and the identified site location plotted against it. (high confidence; objective).

**Jurisdiction / edition:** New York City, New York; NYC Zoning Resolution — text through 2026-08-13

**Project context:** `{}`

**Question:** Within the Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict of the Special Downtown Brooklyn District, what two prerequisites does Section 101-75 state before a demolition permit may be issued, and what unsafe-building exception does it recognize?

### Selected evidence

**ZR 101-04 — canonical section 20020818**

> In order to carry out the provisions of this Chapter, two subdistricts are established within the Special Downtown Brooklyn District. In each of these subdistricts, certain special regulations apply that do not apply within the remainder of the Special Downtown Brooklyn District. The subdistricts are outlined on Map 1 (Special Downtown Brooklyn District and Subdistricts) in Appendix E of this Chapter. Special regulations set forth in Sections 101-60 through 101-632, inclusive, shall apply to the Fulton Mall Subdistrict. Special regulations set forth in Sections 101-70 through 101-75 and Appendices A, B, C and D, inclusive, shall apply to the Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict. The subdistricts are also subject to all other regulations of the Special Downtown Brooklyn District and the underlying districts except as otherwise specified in the subdistrict regulations.

**ZR 101-75 — canonical section 20020889**

> Except in the case of buildings that are unsafe and the demolition of which is required pursuant to the provisions of Chapter 26, Title C, Part I, Article 8, or its successor, of the New York City Administrative Code, no demolition permit shall be issued by the Department of Buildings for any development within the Subdistrict until:

(a) the owner of the property has obtained a building application approval for the new development or enlargement; and

### Permitext answer

Within the Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict, ZR 101-75 bars the Department of Buildings from issuing a demolition permit for a development until both of these prerequisites are met: (1) the owner has obtained building-application approval for the new development or enlargement; and (2) the owner presents evidence of a construction-financing commitment from a domestic bank, insurance company, or real estate investment company, in the form of a letter of trust.

The exception is a building that is unsafe and whose demolition is required under Chapter 26, Title C, Part I, Article 8 (or its successor) of the New York City Administrative Code. In that circumstance, the stated demolition-permit prerequisites do not apply. ZR 101-04 identifies Sections 101-70 through 101-75 as special regulations applicable to the Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict; this answer relies on the stated premise that the development is within that subdistrict.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict scope: The special regulations in Sections 101-70 through 101-75 apply to the Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict, in addition to other applicable Special Downtown Brooklyn District and underlying-district regulations unless otherwise specified.
- Two demolition-permit prerequisites: For development within the Subdistrict, no Department of Buildings demolition permit may be issued until the owner has obtained building-application approval for the new development or enlargement and has presented evidence of a construction-financing commitment from a domestic bank, insurance company, or real estate investment company, in the form of a letter of trust.
- Unsafe-building exception: The demolition-permit restriction does not apply where a building is unsafe and its demolition is required pursuant to Chapter 26, Title C, Part I, Article 8, or its successor, of the New York City Administrative Code.

Special provisions for self-storage facilities In designated areas within Manufacturing Districts, as shown on the maps in APPENDIX J (Designated Areas Within Manufacturing Districts) of this Resolution, a self-service storage facility is subject to the provisions of this Section. Designated areas in which self-service storage facilities are subject to the as-of-right provisions of this paragraph are shown on the maps in Subarea 1, and those in which such uses are subject to special permit of the City Planning Commission pursuant to Section 74-192 (Self-service storage facility in designated areas within Manufacturing Districts) are shown on the maps in Subarea 2. On a zoning lot greater than or equal to 50,000 square feet in area, a self-service storage facility shall provide industrial floor space that is: equal in floor area or cellar space to 25 percent of the lot area; located below the level of the third story, with at least 50 percent of such industrial floor space located on the ground floor, with such ground floor story located within five feet of curb level, or base plane, as applicable, and the remaining industrial floor space located on a level that is immediately above or below such story; and provided with access to freight elevators and the accessory off-street loading berth required for such industrial floor space in accordance with the provisions of Section 44-566 (Regulations for permitted or required loading berths for zoning lots containing self-service storage facilities in designated areas). On a zoning lot that on December 19, 2017, is less than 50,000 square feet in area, a self-service storage facility shall provide: industrial floor space as specified in paragraph (b)(1) of this Section; or floor area or cellar space containing securely subdivided space for lease within such self-service storage facility, where each subdivided space is not less than 100 square feet in area, and with a minimum clear height of eight feet. Such spaces shall be categorized as “business-sized” for the purposes of this Section and the number and sizes of such spaces shall be shown on plans filed with the Department of Buildings. The total area of such business-sized storage space shall be equal in floor area or cellar space to 25 percent of the lot area. On a zoning lot on which industrial floor space is provided in accordance with paragraph (b)(1) or (b)(2)(i) of this Section, an information sign shall be provided. Such required sign shall be mounted on an exterior building wall adjacent to and no more than five feet from all primary entrances of the building containing the industrial floor space. The sign shall be placed so that it is directly visible, without any obstruction, to persons entering the building, and at a height no less than four feet and no more than 5 feet 6 inches above the adjoining grade. Such sign shall be legible, no less than 12 inches by 12 inches in size and shall be fully opaque, non-reflective and constructed of permanent, highly durable materials. The information sign shall contain the name and address of the building in lettering no less than three-quarters of an inch in height, and the following statement in lettering no less than one-half inch in height: “This building is subject to Industrial Floor Space regulations which require a minimum amount of space to be provided for specific industrial uses.” The information sign shall include an Internet URL, or other widely accessible means of electronically transmitting and displaying information to the public, where the information required in paragraph (b)(4) of this Section is available to the public. On a zoning lot on which industrial floor space is provided in accordance with paragraph (b)(1) or (b)(2)(i) of this Section, no later than June 30 of each year, beginning in the first calendar year in which a temporary or final certificate of occupancy was issued for the industrial floor space, the owner of the building subject to the use restrictions of this Section shall prepare a report on the existing conditions of the building. Such report shall be in a form provided by the Director of the Department of City Planning, and shall provide the following information at the designated Internet URL, or other widely accessible means of electronically transmitting and displaying information to the public: the total floor area of the industrial floor space in the building required by this Section; the name of each business establishment occupying floor area reserved for the industrial floor space. Such business establishment name shall include that name by which the establishment does business and is known to the public. For each business establishment, the amount of floor area the Use Group, subgroup and specific use as listed in this Resolution shall also be included; a description of each establishment, using the North American Industry Classification System (NAICS) code and number of employees; the total amount of industrial floor space that is vacant, as applicable; the average annual rent for the portions of the building, in the aggregate, required to be industrial floor space; and the number of new leases executed during the calendar year, categorized by lease duration, in five-year increments from zero to five years, five to 10 years, 10 to 15 years, 15 to 20 years and 20 years or greater. ### Permitext answer

No. On the supplied facts, a property-specific finding that the proposed self-service storage facility is permitted as-of-right cannot be made.

The Use Group IX(A) table shows a self-service storage facility as “♦P in M1, M2, and M3 districts: it is permitted with limitations, not an unqualified use allowance, and it is subject to additional conditions. The P notation also subjects it to the referenced performance standards. Without the property’s mapped zoning district, that table cannot be applied to the property.

For a zoning lot in a designated Manufacturing District area, Appendix J separates the governing path by mapped subarea: Subarea 1 is subject to the as-of-right provisions of Section 42-192, while Subarea 2 is subject to the City Planning Commission special-permit route. Appendix J’s supplied tables identify designated-area names and map numbers, but do not establish the location of this property on an Appendix J boundary or map. A special-district or overlay status likewise cannot be determined from the supplied facts.

If the property is verified to be in Appendix J Subarea 1, the as-of-right path is conditional on the Section 42-192 space requirements. The required amount is 25 percent of the zoning-lot area, measured as floor area or cellar space. Thus, the calculation is zoning-lot area (square feet) × 0.25. For a lot at least 50,000 square feet, the required industrial floor space must also meet the stated story, ground-floor share, curb-level/base-plane, freight-elevator-access, and loading-berth conditions. Only for a zoning lot that was less than 50,000 square feet on December 19, 2017, the specified business-sized-storage alternative is available: each leased subdivided space must be at least 100 square feet with at least an eight-foot clear height, its number and size must be shown on DOB-filed plans, and the aggregate business-sized space must equal 25 percent of lot area. If the property is in Subarea 2, the cited provisions establish a City Planning Commission special-permit path, not an as-of-right finding.

Material missing facts are the address or borough-block-lot; current mapped zoning district; whether the lot is in a Manufacturing District; its exact Appendix J mapped status and Subarea, if any; applicable special-district or overlay status; zoning-lot area in square feet; and the lot’s area on December 19, 2017. If Subarea 1 applies, the proposed industrial-floor-space or business-sized-storage area, layout, story locations, ground-floor allocation, relevant elevation, freight-elevator access, loading-berth information, and—if using the smaller-lot alternative—each lease-space area and clear height are also needed. These unresolved facts can change whether an as-of-right path exists and, if so, whether its conditions are met.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- The basic manufacturing-district allowance is limited: The Use Group IX(A) table lists a self-service storage facility as “♦P in M1, M2, and M3 districts, with parking-requirement category D2. The supplied table legend identifies ♦ as permitted with limitations and P as subject to additional conditions; this is not an unqualified use allowance.
- The P notation invokes performance standards: Except for the listed boat-storage and public-parking uses, every use denoted P in the Section 42-191 table must conform to the performance standards in Sections 42-40 through 42-48. Because the self-service storage facility entry is denoted P, that rule applies.
- Appendix J divides designated areas into two paths: For designated areas within Manufacturing Districts, Appendix J identifies Subarea 1 as subject to the as-of-right provisions and Subarea 2 as subject to the City Planning Commission special-permit route under Section 74-192. The supplied Appendix J tables list designated areas, community districts, and map numbers for each subarea, but do not place the subject property within any mapped boundary.
- Subarea 1 as-of-right use has space and design conditions: In Appendix J Subarea 1, the facility must provide industrial floor space or, only where the zoning lot was less than 50,000 square feet on December 19, 2017, qualifying business-sized storage space. Both applicable alternatives require floor area or cellar space equal to 25 percent of lot area. For lots of at least 50,000 square feet, the industrial-floor-space alternative also has the stated location, ground-floor, elevation, freight-elevator, and loading-berth conditions. For the qualifying smaller-lot business-sized alternative, each securely subdivided leased space must be at least 100 square feet, have an eight-foot minimum clear height, and be identified by number and size on DOB-filed plans.
- Subarea 2 is a Commission special-permit path: For a zoning lot in an Appendix J Subarea 2 designated area, the City Planning Commission may permit the specified development, enlargement, or change of use for a Use Group IX(A) self-service storage facility. The Commission must find the lot appropriate based on the land-use characteristics of the lot and surrounding area, may consider the listed factors, and may impose conditions and safeguards.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- Property address or borough-block-lot and a current authoritative zoning-map determination of the mapped zoning district.
- Whether the zoning lot is in a Manufacturing District and, if so, its precise mapped position within or outside an Appendix J designated-area boundary and within Subarea 1 or Subarea 2.
- Any applicable special-district, subdistrict, or other mapped overlay status that can affect the proposed use.
- Zoning-lot area, in square feet.
- Whether the zoning lot was less than 50,000 square feet on December 19, 2017.
- If Subarea 1 applies: proposed industrial-floor-space or business-sized-storage area in square feet; industrial-space story location, ground-floor share, ground-floor elevation relative to curb level or base plane, freight-elevator access, and loading-berth information; or, for the business-sized alternative, each subdivided lease-space area, clear height, and DOB-filed-plan information.

**Citations**

- ZR 42-192: Establishes the Subarea 1 as-of-right conditions, including the 25-percent-of-lot-area requirement and the December 19, 2017 smaller-lot alternative.

### Permitext answer

No. On the stated premise that the zoning lot contains standard residences, the R7A row of the basic Section 23-22 table sets a 4.00 residential FAR maximum.

42,000 square feet of floor area ÷ 10,000 square feet of zoning-lot area = **4.20 FAR**. The basic allowance is 4.00 × 10,000 square feet = **40,000 square feet**. The stated 42,000 square feet therefore exceeds the basic maximum by **2,000 square feet**, or **0.20 FAR**.

The same R7A row lists **5.01 FAR** for a zoning lot containing qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing—an alternative selected by that qualifying-housing status, not by the stated standard-residence premise. At 5.01 FAR, the numerical allowance would be 50,100 square feet, but that is not the applicable basic comparison on the facts given.

This is only a numerical comparison under the supplied basic table and FAR definition, not an overall zoning-compliance conclusion. Floor area generally means gross floor areas measured from exterior wall faces or building-separation centerlines, subject to the stated inclusions and exclusions. The supplied evidence does not establish whether 42,000 square feet is the final zoning-floor-area figure after those rules, or whether another applicable zoning provision modifies the basic result.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- R7A residential FAR categories: Section 23-22 provides separate maximum residential FARs for standard residences and for qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing. In the R7A row, the maximum is 4.00 FAR for standard residences and 5.01 FAR for qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing. The stated standard-residence condition selects the 4.00 FAR category for this basic comparison.
- FAR arithmetic: FAR is total floor area on the zoning lot divided by zoning-lot area. Using 42,000 square feet of floor area and 10,000 square feet of lot area yields 4.20 FAR. At the applicable 4.00 FAR basic maximum, the allowance is 40,000 square feet, so the stated amount exceeds it by 2,000 square feet, or 0.20 FAR.
- Floor-area input must follow the definition: Floor area is generally the sum of gross floor areas measured from exterior wall faces or centerlines between buildings. The definition both includes specified spaces and excludes specified spaces under stated conditions; therefore, a reported area must be evaluated under those provisions before it can be confirmed as the FAR numerator.

- None

**ZR 27-111 — canonical section 20022699**

> unds may be set forth in the guidelines.

Affordable housing regulatory agreement

An “affordable housing regulatory agreement” is a legally binding agreement between the owner of a building and a Federal, State, or local agency or instrumentality with respect to a development, enlargement, or conversion from non-residential to residential use, which:

requires all of the dwelling units, rooming units, or supportive housing units in such building to be income-restricted and occupied by an eligible household as established by such agreement for a period of no less than 30 years; for a rental building, restricts an amount of floor area that would otherwise be required for the MIH development, UAP development or qualifying residential site subject to affordability requirements for the life of such building, or, for a homeownership building, requires such building to be owned by a housing development fund corporation established pursuant to Article XI of the Private Housing Finance Law for the life of such building; and is entered into in connection with public funding.

HPD may impose additional requirements for buildings subject to an affordable housing regulatory agreement in the guidelines.

Affordable housing unit

An “affordable housing unit” is:

a dwelling unit, other than a super’s unit, that is used for class A occupancy as defined in the Multiple Dwelling Law, or a rooming unit, other than a super’s unit, that is used for either Class A or Class B occupancy as defined in the Multiple Dwelling Law, and that is or will be restricted, pursuant to an affordable housing regulatory agreement or restrictive declaration, to occupancy by: for a UAP site: households having an income less than or equal to a weighted average of 60 percent of the income index at initial occupancy: with no more than three income bands; no income band exceeding 100 percent of the income index; and for UAP sites with 10,000 square feet or more of affordable floor area, at least 20 percent of such affordable floor area at an income band of no more than 40 percent of the income index. However, with regard to preservation affordable housing, a grandfathered tenant shall also be permitted to occupy such affordable housing unit; or households as specified in an affordable housing regulatory agreement executed after December 5, 2024; or for an MIH site, qualifying households; a supportive housing unit within a supportive housing project.

Affordable housing units that are restricted to homeownership, as defined in Section 27-113, pursuant to an affordable housing regulatory agreement or a restrictive declaration, must be dwelling units.

Capital element

“Capital elements” are, with respect to any UAP site, the electrical, plumbing, heating and ventilation systems in such UAP site, any air conditioning system in such UAP site and all facades, parapets, roofs, windows, doors, elevators, concrete and masonry in such UAP site and any other portions of such UAP site specified in the guidelines.

Completion notice

A “completion notice” is a notice from HPD to the Department of Buildings stating that the affordable housing in all or a portion of any MIH site or UAP site is complete and stating the affordable floor area of such affordable housing.

Eligible common area

An eligible common area includes any residential floor area that is located within a super’s unit, and any residential floor area in such MIH site or UAP site that is not located within any other dwelling unit or rooming unit, but shall not include any residential floor area for which a user fee is charged to residents of affordable housing units.

Grandfathered tenant

A “grandfathered tenant” is any household that:

occupied an affordable housing unit in preservation affordable housing on the restrictive declaration date, pursuant to a lease, occupancy agreement or statutory tenancy under which one or more members of such household was a primary tenant of such affordable housing unit; and has not been certified to have an annual income below the income band applicable to such affordable housing unit; or in homeownership preservation affordable housing, has been certified to have an annual income below the income band applicable to such affordable housing unit, but has elected not to purchase such affordable housing unit.

In Mandatory Inclusionary Housing areas, grandfathered tenants may include tenants of buildings on an MIH site that have been or will be demolished, as set forth in the guidelines.
> claration, without regard to the date of any amendment.

Super’s unit

A “super’s unit” is, in any MIH site or UAP site, not more than one dwelling unit or rooming unit that is reserved for occupancy by the superintendent of such building.

UAP development

A “UAP development” (“Universal Affordability Preference development”) is a development or enlargement outside of a Mandatory Inclusionary Housing area that provides affordable housing or a supportive housing project that satisfies the requirements of this Chapter.

The residential floor area ratio in a UAP development may exceed that for standard residences set forth in Section 23-22 (Floor Area Regulations for R6 Through R12 Districts) only by the amount of affordable housing provided, either on the UAP zoning lot or, for UAP developments within a UAP Offsite Option Area, on a UAP site pursuant to paragraph (a) of Section 27-16 (Requirements for MIH Sites or UAP Sites).

However, UAP developments within a UAP Offsite Option Area may exceed the floor area ratios for standard residences set forth in Section 23-22 by utilizing affordable housing provided on a generating site, as such term was defined prior to December 5, 2024, at the rate set forth in Section 23-154, as such Section existed prior to December 5, 2024, provided that such generating site has vested pursuant to the provisions of Section 27-132.

UAP Offsite Option Area

A “UAP offsite option area” (“Universal Affordability Preference offsite option area”) is a former Inclusionary Housing Designated Area or R10 District outside of a former Inclusionary Housing Designated Area within which the limited UAP offsite option is applicable, pursuant to the regulations set forth for such areas in Section 27-00, inclusive. The locations of former Inclusionary Housing Designated Areas are identified in APPENDIX F of this Resolution.

UAP site

A “UAP site” (“Universal Affordability Preference site”) is a building that contains affordable housing or a supportive housing project for a UAP development

Any temporary or final certificate of occupancy issued after December 5, 2024, for a UAP site shall state that such building or portion thereof contains affordable housing, and shall state that such certificate of occupancy may be amended or superseded to reflect that the residential units in the building or portion thereof that are affordable housing units be used other than as affordable housing units only in accordance with the provisions of this Zoning Resolution.

UAP zoning lot

A “UAP zoning lot” (“Universal Affordability Preference zoning lot”) is a zoning lot that contains a UAP development and utilizes the floor area regulations of Section 23-22 (Floor Area Regulations for R6 Through R12 Districts) or the height and setback regulations of Section 23-432 (Height and setback requirements) applicable to qualifying affordable housing.

**ZR 27-16 — canonical section 20022711**

> Affordable housing in an MIH site or UAP site shall meet each of the requirements set forth in this Section for the entire regulatory period, except that affordable housing restricted pursuant to an affordable housing regulatory agreement shall only be required to comply with paragraphs (a) and (j) of this Section.

Location of MIH site or UAP site and MIH zoning lot or UAP zoning lot Where an MIH site or UAP site is not located within the MIH zoning lot or the UAP zoning lot, as applicable: the MIH site or UAP site and the MIH zoning lot or UAP development, as applicable, shall be located within the same Community District; or the MIH site or UAP site and the MIH zoning lot or UAP zoning lot, as applicable, shall be located in adjacent Community Districts and within one-half mile of each other, measured from the perimeter of each zoning lot. Outside of UAP offsite option areas, a UAP site must be located within the UAP zoning lot. Distribution of affordable housing units In new construction affordable housing, where one or more of the dwelling units or rooming units in an MIH site or UAP site, other than any super’s unit, are not affordable housing units: the affordable housing units shall be distributed on not less than 65 percent of the residential stories of such MIH site or UAP site, or, if there are insufficient affordable housing units to comply with this requirement, the distribution of affordable housing units shall be as specified in the guidelines; and not more than two-thirds of the dwelling units and rooming units on any story of such MIH site or UAP site shall be affordable housing units, unless not less than two-thirds of the dwelling units and rooming units on each residential story of such MIH site or UAP site are affordable housing units. HPD may waive such requirement for any new construction affordable housing that is located on an interior lot or through lot with less than 50 feet of frontage along any street.

Where one or more of the dwelling units or rooming units, other than any super’s unit, are not affordable housing units, the affordable housing units shall share a common primary entrance with the other dwelling units or rooming units. However, if an MIH site or UAP site contains both homeownership and rental housing and no affordable housing units are homeownership housing, the distribution requirements above shall only apply to residential stories containing rental housing. In addition, the distribution requirements above shall not apply if all affordable housing units are rental affordable housing and all other dwelling units are homeownership housing, and shall not apply to any affordable housing units that are also supportive housing units or affordable independent residences for seniors.

In addition, HPD may waive these requirements for affordable floor area created in an MIH site or UAP site through enlargement, as specified in the guidelines.

HPD may disapprove any building configuration that would frustrate the intent and purpose of this Section by segregating affordable housing units or stigmatizing residents of such affordable housing units.

Bedroom mix of affordable housing units In new construction affordable housing, where one or more of the dwelling units in an MIH site or UAP site, other than any super’s unit, are not affordable housing units, either: the dwelling units that are affordable housing units shall contain a bedroom mix at least proportional to the bedroom mix of the dwelling units, other than any super’s unit, that are not affordable housing units; or not less than 50 percent of the dwelling units that are affordable housing units shall contain two or more bedrooms and not less than 75 percent of the dwelling units that are affordable housing units shall contain one or more bedrooms. However, such bedroom mix requirements shall not apply to affordable independent residences for seniors. HPD may also waive such

### Permitext answer

Section 23-22 establishes these numerical ceilings for the stated 10,000-square-foot R7A zoning lot: **4.00 FAR** for standard residences and **5.01 FAR** for qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing. The arithmetic is:

- 10,000 sq ft × 4.00 = **40,000 sq ft** residential floor area.
- 10,000 sq ft × 5.01 = **50,100 sq ft** residential floor area.
- The proposed **48,000 sq ft** is **8,000 sq ft above** the standard-residence ceiling and **2,100 sq ft below** the qualifying-affordable/senior table ceiling.

Thus, the table establishes a numerical maximum by district and category; it does not itself establish that this project is entitled to use the 5.01 category. The description that the lot contains qualifying affordable housing is not enough by itself. That term includes only an MIH development in a Mandatory Inclusionary Housing area, a UAP development, or a building subject to an affordable housing regulatory agreement.

If the route is UAP, the development must be outside an MIH area and provide affordable housing or a supportive-housing project satisfying Chapter 27. Its FAR may exceed the 4.00 standard-residence FAR only by the amount of affordable housing provided. On the stated arithmetic, the 8,000-sq-ft difference is therefore the amount implied by that relationship to reach 48,000 sq ft—but the supplied text does not establish the detailed counting method or establish that any particular 8,000 sq ft qualifies. The affordable-floor-area amount and qualification must be confirmed.

For a UAP site, the affordable units must meet the supplied definition. Among other applicable details, UAP units must be restricted under an affordable housing regulatory agreement or restrictive declaration; at initial occupancy, household incomes must have a weighted average no greater than 60 percent of the income index, with no more than three income bands and none above 100 percent. If the UAP site has **10,000 sq ft or more of affordable floor area**, at least **20 percent of that affordable floor area** must be at an income band no greater than 40 percent of the income index. A completion notice, if issued, states the completed affordable floor area.

The selected MIH/UAP-site text also requires compliance for the regulatory period, subject to its stated exception for affordable housing restricted under an affordable housing regulatory agreement. For new construction with both affordable and non-affordable units, the supplied text addresses distribution across at least 65 percent of residential stories, the two-thirds per-story limit and its stated alternative, a common primary entrance, and either a proportional bedroom mix or the stated 50-percent two-bedroom/75-percent one-bedroom alternative. Whether those requirements apply or are excepted depends on unresolved facts, including rental versus homeownership configuration, whether affordable units are supportive housing or affordable independent residences for seniors, interior/through-lot frontage and any HPD waiver, and whether affordable floor area is created through enlargement. The supplied text also states specific distribution exceptions for the listed homeownership/rental and supportive/senior situations.

Before concluding that 5.01 FAR is available, confirm: the specific qualifying route; if MIH, that the lot is in an MIH area; if UAP, that it is outside an MIH area; the exact affordable floor area and the legal restrictions, income-band schedule, and regulatory-period compliance supporting its qualification; and whether the affordable floor area is on the UAP zoning lot. Outside a UAP Offsite Option Area, the UAP site must be within the UAP zoning lot. If an offsite route is proposed, the record must establish the applicable UAP Offsite Option Area status and the supplied Community District/distance conditions. If the route is an affordable housing regulatory agreement, the executed agreement must establish its agency counterparty, public-funding connection, income restrictions and eligible-household occupancy, and the required duration. Accordingly, 48,000 sq ft is numerically below the 5.01 table ceiling, but the higher FAR cannot yet be concluded available on the facts supplied.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

With a stated zoning-lot area of 10,000 square feet, those values yield 40,000 square feet and 50,100 square feet, respectively. The proposed 48,000 square feet is 8,000 square feet above the standard-residence ceiling and 2,100 square feet below the qualifying-affordable/senior ceiling. This is a numerical table comparison, not an independent determination of overall zoning entitlement.

A statement that a project contains affordable housing does not alone establish one of these defined routes.
- UAP increase is bounded by affordable housing provided: A UAP development is outside a Mandatory Inclusionary Housing area and provides affordable housing or a supportive housing project satisfying Chapter 27. Its residential FAR may exceed the standard-residence FAR only by the amount of affordable housing provided. On the supplied numbers, 8,000 square feet is the arithmetic difference between the standard 40,000-square-foot ceiling and the proposed 48,000 square feet; whether that amount is qualifying affordable floor area requires confirmation under the applicable rules. Outside UAP Offsite Option Areas, the UAP site must be within the UAP zoning lot; the selected text also describes limited offsite paths and the required location relationship where an MIH or UAP site is not within the related zoning lot.

### Expected conclusion

Section 23-22 sets an R7A maximum FAR of 5.01 for qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing. A 5.01 FAR applied to 10,000 square feet permits 50,100 square feet, so the stated 48,000 square feet is below that table ceiling. Being below the table ceiling does not establish entitlement to the higher FAR; the applicable MIH, UAP, or regulatory-agreement path and its required amount of affordable floor area must be established under Article II, Chapter 7. For a UAP path, Section 27-111 limits the increase above the standard-residence FAR to the amount of affordable housing provided, so the project description alone cannot establish that the full proposed 4.80 FAR is available. The user's label alone is not treated as proof that the development qualifies for the higher FAR.

### Permitext answer

No. Under the supplied basic standard-lot rule for R7 districts, an interior lot is limited to 80% residential lot coverage. The calculation is 10,000 square feet × 0.80 = 8,000 square feet. Proposed coverage of 8,500 square feet is 85% of the lot and exceeds the basic maximum by 500 square feet, or 5 percentage points.

That is the direct result under the basic rule, not a determination of every possible conditional coverage adjustment. For a qualifying shallow interior lot, coverage may increase 1% for each 5 feet that the lot depth is below 95 feet, but may not exceed 90%. If a front or rear lot line meets a side lot line at an angle other than 90 degrees, depth is measured at the midpoint of that angled lot line. A shallow portion may exceed the 90% maximum only if the adjusted maximum coverage for the zoning lot complies with that 90% cap. Separate 100% rules apply only to the specified portions within 100 feet of a qualifying street intersection or, where the front lot line coincides with the street line along the short block dimension, within 100 feet of that street line.

The shallow-lot coverage adjustment depends on eligibility for the supplied shallow rear-yard modification. Standard rear yards remain required on interior lots: for detached or zero-lot-line buildings, at least 20 feet at or below 75 feet in height measured from base plane, and 30 feet above 75 feet where permitted; for semi-detached or attached buildings, at least 30 feet where lot width is under 40 feet, or—where lot width is 40 feet or more—20 feet at or below 75 feet and 30 feet above 75 feet where permitted. The shallow modification requires an interior lot less than 95 feet deep at some point, with that condition existing on December 15, 1961 and not subsequently changing in depth; it allows a six-inch rear-yard reduction per foot below 95 feet, but never below 10 feet.

The supplied eligible-site coverage rule also does not support 85% coverage on this stated lot: its 65% category applies to sites of at least 30,000 square feet that are not large sites, and its large-site category is 50%.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Basic interior-lot coverage maximum: For standard lots in the stated R6 through R12 district group, maximum residential lot coverage is 80% for interior or through lots and 100% for corner lots. On the stated 10,000-square-foot interior lot, 80% is 8,000 square feet; 8,500 square feet is 85%, exceeding the basic maximum by 500 square feet.
- Conditional increases for interior or through lots: The otherwise applicable maximum may increase for a lot eligible for the specified shallow-lot rear-yard modification by 1% for every 5 feet of depth below 95 feet for an interior lot, subject to a 90% maximum. For an irregularly angled front or rear lot line, depth is measured at the midpoint of that lot line. Shallow portions may exceed 90% only if adjusted maximum lot coverage complies with the 90% cap. The section separately provides 100% coverage for qualifying portions within 100 feet of a street intersection of 135 degrees or less and, under the stated short-block-dimension condition, within 100 feet of that street line.
- Standard and shallow-lot rear-yard rules: Interior lots must provide rear yards. For detached and zero-lot-line buildings, the standard rear yard is at least 20 feet for portions at or below 75 feet measured from base plane and 30 feet for portions above 75 feet where permitted. For semi-detached and attached buildings, it is at least 30 feet if lot width is under 40 feet; if width is 40 feet or more, it is 20 feet at or below 75 feet and 30 feet above 75 feet where permitted. A shallow interior-lot modification requires depth below 95 feet at some point, that condition to have existed on December 15, 1961 without a later depth increase or decrease, and permits a six-inch reduction per foot of depth shortfall, never reducing the yard below 10 feet.

- Whether any proposed covered portion is within 100 feet of the intersection of two street lines meeting at an angle of 135 degrees or less.
- Whether the front lot line coincides with a street line along the short dimension of the block, and whether any proposed covered portion is within 100 feet of that street line.

For semi-detached and attached buildings on zoning lots less than 40 feet wide, the required rear-yard depth is at least 30 feet at every rear lot line. The proposed yard is therefore 10 feet short.

A separate shallow-lot modification may apply if the interior lot is less than 95 feet deep at any point, the shallow condition existed on December 15, 1961, and its depth has neither increased nor decreased since. The standard requirement may then be reduced by 6 inches per foot below 95 feet, but not below 10 feet. Because the stated lot width is 35 feet, the proposed 20-foot rear yard is 10 feet below that standard minimum.
- Conditional shallow-lot reduction: For an interior lot less than 95 feet deep at any point, the standard rear-yard depth may be reduced by 6 inches for each foot by which the lot depth is below 95 feet only where the shallow condition existed on December 15, 1961 and has neither increased nor decreased in depth since. The resulting required yard cannot be reduced below 10 feet.

No—not on the supplied facts. A 20-foot rear yard equivalent is permitted for a through lot that is less than 190 feet deep only if the shallow-lot condition existed on December 15, 1961 and has not subsequently increased or decreased in depth. Because that historical condition is unknown, the supplied provision does not establish entitlement to the shallow-lot reduction.

If that history is documented, the calculation for a 150-foot-deep lot is: 190 feet − 150 feet = 40 feet of permitted reduction. Applied to the stated standard depths—40 feet at or below 75 feet in height, or 60 feet above 75 feet where permitted—the result would reach the stated 20-foot minimum in either case. The 20-foot proposal would still need to satisfy the applicable location and unobstructed-to-sky requirements. If the historical condition cannot be established, the supplied text does not provide a basis to treat 20 feet as sufficient.

This conclusion also assumes that no listed exception removes rear-yard-equivalent regulation for the zoning lot.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Historic shallow-lot condition is required for the reduction: For a through lot that is less than 190 feet deep at any point, the rear-yard-equivalent depth may be reduced only where the shallow-lot condition existed on December 15, 1961 and has neither increased nor decreased in depth thereafter.
- Reduction calculation and 20-foot floor: For a qualifying shallow through lot, the standard rear-yard-equivalent depth may be reduced by one foot for each foot by which the zoning lot, or portion, is less than 190 feet deep, but the required yard or portion cannot be reduced below 20 feet. At a depth of 150 feet, the reduction is 40 feet (190 feet minus 150 feet).
- Standard depths and physical conditions: The stated standard depth is 40 feet for buildings or portions at or below 75 feet in height and 60 feet above 75 feet where permitted. A rear yard equivalent must be located midway, or within 10 feet of midway, between the street lines unless an expressly stated alternative-location allowance applies, and it must be unobstructed from its lowest level to the sky except as allowed by the referenced obstruction provision.

**Assumptions**

- Whether the zoning lot occupies an entire block.
- The proposed rear-yard-equivalent location and whether it is unobstructed from its lowest level to the sky, subject to any permitted obstructions.

**ZR 77-02 — canonical section 20018891**

> Whenever a zoning lot is divided by a boundary between two or more districts and such zoning lot did not exist on December 15, 1961, or any applicable subsequent amendment thereto, each portion of such zoning lot shall be regulated by all the provisions applicable to the district in which such portion of the zoning lot is located. However, the provisions of Section 77-22 (Floor Area Ratio) shall apply to zoning lots created at any time where different bulk regulations apply to different portions of such zoning lot.

**ZR 77-11 — canonical section 20018894**

> Whenever a zoning lot existing on December 15, 1961, or on any applicable subsequent amendment thereto, is divided by a boundary between districts in which different uses are permitted, the use regulations applicable to the district in which more than 50 percent of the lot area of the zoning lot is located may apply to the entire zoning lot, provided that the greatest distance from the mapped district boundary to any lot line of such zoning lot in the district in which less than 50 percent of its area is located does not exceed 25 feet. Such distance shall be measured perpendicular to the mapped district boundary.

Whenever the use regulations are so applied, the district boundary may be assumed to be relocated accordingly, and the bulk, off-street parking and loading, and all other regulations applying to such expanded district shall apply to the entire zoning lot. However, when the zoning lot is divided by a district boundary between a district limited to single- or two-family residences and a district permitting multiple dwellings, the use and bulk regulations of an R3-2 District shall apply in the R1, R2, R3A, R3X or R3-1 portion, and the use and bulk regulations of an R4 District shall apply in the R2X, R4A, R4-1 or R4B portion.

Except as specifically provided by the provisions of a special purpose district, the provisions of this Section shall apply to zoning lots which are divided by a special purpose district boundary line.

**ZR 77-22 — canonical section 20018899**

> The maximum floor area ratio permitted on each portion of such zoning lot for the applicable type of building or buildings on such zoning lot shall be determined under the applicable regulations of this Resolution.

Each such floor area ratio shall be multiplied by the percentage of the zoning lot to which such floor area ratio applies. The sum of the products thus obtained shall be the adjusted maximum floor area ratio applicable to such zoning lot.

The floor area resulting from application of the adjusted maximum floor area ratio may be located anywhere on the zoning lot, subject to all other regulations of this Resolution, and provided that the floor area ratio for any portion of the zoning lot within one district shall not exceed the maximum floor area ratio, specified for that district, or the adjusted maximum floor area ratio for the zoning lot, whichever is greater, except that the portion of the zoning lot fronting on and within 100 feet of a wide street and permitting the greater maximum permitted residential floor area ratio may exceed the maximum permitted residential floor area ratio for the portion of the zoning lot by up to 20 percent.

In applying the provisions of this Section, the following conditions shall apply:

the floor area bonus permitted for publicly accessible open areas or arcades, under the applicable regulations of this Resolution, shall apply only to such publicly accessible open areas, arcades or portions thereof, as are located in a district in which such bonus is granted; and when a zoning lot contains a sky exposure plane building which does not have a specified maximum floor area ratio, for the purpose of computing the adjusted maximum floor area ratio, the floor area ratio of such zoning lot shall be deemed to be that which can be achieved at the minimum required open space ratio for such zoning lot.

### Permitext answer

No—not automatically. More than 50 percent of the lot area being in the less restrictive district is only one condition in ZR 77-11. That section applies only if the zoning lot existed on December 15, 1961, or on the applicable subsequent zoning-amendment date; the districts permit different uses; and the greatest perpendicular distance from the mapped district boundary to any lot line in the minority-area portion is no more than 25 feet. A 2026 assembly alone does not establish whether the lot existed on the pertinent amendment date, so eligibility for the whole-lot option cannot be determined from the supplied facts.

If ZR 77-11 does apply, the majority district's regulations may apply—not automatically must apply—to the entire lot. The boundary may then be treated as relocated, and the expanded district's bulk, off-street parking, loading, and other regulations apply across the lot. The stated rule is subject to its special residential-boundary proviso: for a boundary between a district limited to single- or two-family residences and one permitting multiple dwellings, the specified R3-2 or R4 regulations apply in the listed lower-density residential portions. It also applies across a special-purpose-district boundary unless that special district specifically provides otherwise.

If the lot did not exist on December 15, 1961 or an applicable subsequent amendment date, ZR 77-02 requires each district portion to be regulated by all provisions applicable to that portion. But the supplied fact that the lot was assembled in 2026 does not, by itself, prove that historical condition.

FAR is separate. Where different bulk regulations apply to portions of a lot created at any time, ZR 77-22 uses a weighted adjusted FAR: determine each portion's applicable FAR, multiply it by that portion's percentage of total lot area, and add the products. Resulting floor area may be located anywhere on the lot subject to all other regulations, but the FAR in a district portion ordinarily cannot exceed the greater of that district's maximum FAR or the lot's adjusted maximum FAR. A portion fronting on and within 100 feet of a wide street that permits the greater maximum residential FAR may exceed that portion's residential FAR by up to 20 percent. Publicly accessible open-area or arcade bonuses apply only to the portions located in a district that grants the bonus. For a sky-exposure-plane building without a specified maximum FAR, the FAR used in the adjusted-FAR calculation is deemed to be the FAR achievable at the minimum required open-space ratio. None of these FAR rules extends the majority district's use regulations.

This applies the supplied continuously amended Zoning Resolution text, not historical text as of an earlier date.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Section 77-11 does not make majority area alone sufficient: ZR 77-11 allows the use regulations of the district containing more than 50 percent of lot area to apply to an entire divided zoning lot only when the lot existed on December 15, 1961, or on an applicable subsequent amendment date; the districts permit different uses; and the greatest perpendicular distance from the mapped boundary to any lot line in the minority-area district portion is no more than 25 feet. The word may makes this a permitted result, not an automatic one. The supplied fact that the lot was assembled in 2026 does not establish whether it existed on the relevant applicable amendment date.
- Nonqualifying lots are regulated by district portion: For a zoning lot divided by district boundaries that did not exist on December 15, 1961, or on an applicable subsequent amendment date, ZR 77-02 requires each portion to be regulated by all provisions applicable to the district in which that portion is located. Whether that rule governs this lot remains conditional because the supplied facts do not establish the relevant amendment history or existence date.
- A valid whole-lot use election carries stated consequences and limits: When ZR 77-11 is validly used, the district boundary may be assumed relocated and the expanded district's bulk, off-street parking, loading, and other regulations apply across the lot. The section contains a distinct proviso for specified boundaries between single-/two-family districts and multiple-dwelling districts, and it applies across a special-purpose-district boundary except where a special-purpose-district provision specifically provides otherwise.
- FAR uses a separate weighted calculation: Where bulk regulations differ between portions of a lot created at any time, ZR 77-22 calculates adjusted maximum FAR by multiplying each portion's applicable FAR by its percentage of total lot area and adding the products. Floor area may be located anywhere subject to all other regulations, but a district portion ordinarily may not exceed the greater of its district maximum FAR or the adjusted maximum FAR. The passage separately provides a limited up-to-20-percent wide-street residential FAR allowance, restricts open-area or arcade bonuses to portions in districts granting them, and supplies a sky-exposure-plane deeming rule for a building with no specified maximum FAR. This FAR method does not authorize extending majority-district use regulations.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- The applicable subsequent zoning amendment date and evidence whether this zoning lot existed on that date.
- Official zoning-map and survey evidence establishing the exact mapped district boundary, each district portion's area, and the greatest perpendicular distance from that boundary to every lot line in the minority-area portion.
- Whether the two mapped districts permit different uses.
- Whether the boundary is a special-purpose-district boundary and, if so, the enacted special-purpose-district provision that specifically modifies ZR 77-11.
- For any FAR calculation: the applicable building type, applicable FAR for each district portion, each portion's percentage of total lot area, whether a qualifying portion fronts on and is within 100 feet of a wide street, whether a publicly accessible open-area or arcade bonus is involved, and whether a sky-exposure-plane building without a specified maximum FAR is involved.

**Citations**

- ZR 77-02: ZR 77-02 supplies the portion-by-portion rule for a divided zoning lot that did not exist on the stated baseline or applicable amendment date.
- ZR 77-11: ZR 77-11 supplies the pre-existing-lot, different-use, majority-area, 25-foot measurement, special residential proviso, and special-purpose-district conditions for whole-lot treatment.
- ZR 77-22: ZR 77-22 supplies the separate adjusted-FAR calculation, portion cap, wide-street exception, bonus limitation, and sky-exposure-plane deeming rule.

Section 77-11's majority-district use treatment applies only to qualifying zoning lots existing on December 15, 1961 or on the date of an applicable subsequent amendment. For the stated lot created in 2026, Section 77-02 generally regulates each portion under the district in which it is located. Section 77-22 separately applies an adjusted maximum FAR calculation to divided zoning lots created at any time. The answer distinguishes use-regulation treatment from the separate adjusted-FAR mechanism.

**ZR 12-10 — canonical section 20018523**

> zoning lot General Definition Last Amended 2/2/2011 A "zoning lot" is either: (a) a lot of record existing on December 15, 1961, or any applicable subsequent amendment thereto; (b) a tract of land, either unsubdivided or consisting of two or more contiguous lots of record, located within a single block, which, on December 15, 1961, or any applicable subsequent amendment thereto, was in single ownership; (c) a tract of land, either unsubdivided or consisting of two or more lots of record contiguous for a minimum of 10 linear feet, located within a single block, which at the time of filing for a building permit (or, if no building permit is required, at the time of the filing for a certificate of occupancy) is under single fee ownership and with respect to which each party having any interest therein is a party in interest (as defined herein); or (d) a tract of land, either unsubdivided or consisting of two or more lots of record contiguous for a minimum of 10 linear feet, located within a single block, which at the time of filing for a building permit (or, if no building permit is required, at the time of filing for a certificate of occupancy) is declared to be a tract of land to be treated as one zoning lot for the purpose of this Resolution. A zoning lot, therefore, may or may not coincide with a lot as shown on the official tax map of the City of New York, or on any recorded subdivision plat or deed. (e) For purposes of the provisions of paragraph (c) hereof: (1) prior to issuing a building permit or a certificate of occupancy, as the case may be, the Department of Buildings shall be furnished with a certificate issued to the applicant therefor by a title insurance company licensed to do business in the State of New York showing that each party having any interest in the subject tract of land is a party in interest (as defined herein); except that where the City of New York is a fee owner, such certificate may be issued by the New York City Law Department; and (2) a "party in interest" in the tract of land shall include only (W) the fee owner thereof, (X) the holder of any enforceable recorded interest superior to that of the fee owner and which could result in such holder obtaining possession of all or substantially all of such tract of land, (Y) the holder of any enforceable recorded interest in all or substantially all of such tract of land which would be adversely affected by the development thereof and (Z) the holder of any unrecorded interest in all or substantially all of such tract of land which would be superior to and adversely affected by the development thereof and which would be disclosed by a physical inspection of the tract of land. except that where the City of New York is a fee owner, such certificate may be issued by the New York City Law Department; (2) the Buildings Department, in issuing a building permit for construction of a building or other structure on the zoning lot declared pursuant to paragraph (d) above or, if no building permit is required, in issuing a certificate of occupancy for such building or other structure, shall accept an application for same from and, if all conditions for issuance of same are fulfilled, shall issue same to any party to the Declaration; (3) by their execution and recording of a Declaration, the parties to the Declaration, and all parties who have waived their respective rights to execute such Declaration, shall be deemed to have agreed that no breach by any party to the Declaration, or any agreement ancillary thereto, shall have any effect on the treatment of the tract of land covered by the Declaration as one zoning lot for purposes of this Resolution and such tract of land shall be treated as one zoning lot unless such zoning lot is subdivided in accordance with the provisions of this Resolution; and (4) a "party in interest" in the portion of the tract of land covered by a Declaration shall include only (W) the fee owner or owners thereof, (X) the holder of any enforceable recorded interest in all or part thereof which would be superior to the Declaration and which could result in such holder obtaining possession of any portion of such tract of land, (Y) the holder of any enforceable recorded interest in all or part thereof which would be adversely affected by the Declaration, and (Z) the holder of any unrecorded interest in all or part thereof which would be superior to and adversely affected by the Declaration and which would be disclosed by a physical inspection of the portion of the tract of land covered by the Declaration. A zoning lot may be subdivided into two or more zoning lots, provided that all resulting zoning lots and all buildings thereon shall comply with all of the applicable provisions of this Resolution. If such zoning lot, however, is occupied by a non-complying building, such zoning lot may be subdivided provided such subdivision does not create a new non-compliance or increase the degree of non-compliance of such building. Where ownership of a zoning lot or portion thereof was effected prior to the effective date of this amendment, as evidenced by an attorney's affidavit, any development, enlargement or alteration on such zoning lot may be based upon such prior effected ownership as then defined in the zoning lot definition of Section 12-10. Such prior leasehold agreements shall be duly recorded prior to August 1, 1978. Prior to the issuance of any permit for a development or enlargement pursuant to this Resolution a complete metes and bounds of the zoning lot, the tax lot number, the block number and the ownership of the zoning lot as set forth in paragraphs (a), (b), (c) and (d) herein shall be recorded by the applicant in the Conveyances Section of the Office of the City Register (or, if applicable, the County Clerk's Office) of the county in which the said zoning lot is located. The zoning lot definition in effect prior to the effective date of this amendment shall continue to apply to Board of Standards and Appeals approvals in effect at the effective date hereof.

### Permitext answer

No—not under the current single-fee-ownership route in the supplied general definition. That route requires component lots to be within one block, under single fee ownership at the applicable permit or certificate-of-occupancy filing, and contiguous for a minimum of 10 linear feet. An 8-linear-foot common boundary is 2 linear feet short of that minimum. The Declaration of Restrictions route has the same 10-linear-foot contiguity minimum, so a declaration does not cure the stated deficiency.

Two separate qualifications remain. First, each lot may independently be a zoning lot if it is a qualifying lot of record under the historical lot-of-record definition; that does not itself combine the two lots into one zoning lot. Second, a tract of contiguous lots within one block that was in single ownership on December 15, 1961, or an applicable subsequent amendment date, is a separate historical route and the supplied text does not impose the 10-foot minimum on that route. The statement that the lots were not historically one zoning lot does not by itself establish whether they were in single ownership on the relevant historical date. If they were not, present common ownership alone cannot combine the 8-foot-connected lots under the supplied definition.

If relying on the current single-fee-ownership route for a permit or certificate of occupancy, DOB must receive a title-insurance certificate showing that every party having an interest in the tract is a qualifying party in interest. If relying on the declaration route, DOB instead must receive a title-insurance certificate showing that required parties executed the declaration and that the declaration and any waivers were recorded. For a development or enlargement permit, the complete metes and bounds, tax-lot and block numbers, and ownership also must be recorded. The supplied evidence does not establish the lots' mapped zoning or special-purpose-district status, so it does not establish whether a site-specific enacted provision modifies this general rule.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Current common ownership does not overcome the 10-foot minimum: The current single-fee-ownership route for two or more lots of record requires that they be located within one block, contiguous for a minimum of 10 linear feet, under single fee ownership at the applicable building-permit or certificate-of-occupancy filing, and that each party having an interest be a party in interest. The stated 8 linear feet of contact does not meet that minimum.
- The declaration route also requires 10 feet of contiguity: The Declaration of Restrictions route likewise applies only to two or more lots of record within one block that are contiguous for a minimum of 10 linear feet. It further requires declarations covering the entire tract, execution by each required party in interest or a recorded waiver, and recording against the affected lots. Thus, that route does not establish a means to combine lots with only 8 linear feet of contact.
- Historical routes remain distinct: A qualifying lot of record existing on December 15, 1961, or an applicable subsequent amendment date, may independently be a zoning lot. Separately, contiguous lots within one block may qualify as one historical tract if they were in single ownership on December 15, 1961, or an applicable subsequent amendment date. The supplied text does not state a 10-linear-foot minimum for this historical-single-ownership route.
- Permit-path title certificates and recording differ by route: For the current single-fee-ownership route, DOB must receive a title-insurance certificate showing that each party having an interest is a party in interest. For the declaration route, DOB must receive a title-insurance certificate showing execution by the required parties and recording of the declaration and any waivers. Before issuance of a development or enlargement permit, the zoning lot's complete metes and bounds, tax-lot number, block number, and ownership must be recorded.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- Whether the tract was in single ownership on December 15, 1961, or on an applicable subsequent amendment date, for the separate historical-single-ownership route.
- Whether each individual tax lot was a lot of record on December 15, 1961, or an applicable subsequent amendment date, for its independent zoning-lot status.
- The lots' mapped zoning district and whether an applicable Special Purpose District contains an enacted provision modifying the general ZR 12-10 definition.
- If a current single-fee-ownership permit or certificate-of-occupancy filing is proposed, the title-insurance certificate establishing every relevant party having an interest and that each is a qualifying party in interest.
- If a declaration route is proposed despite the contiguity issue, the executed and recorded declarations or waivers and the title-insurance certificate showing their execution and recording.
- If a development or enlargement permit is proposed, the recorded complete metes-and-bounds description, tax-lot number, block number, and ownership information.

> cellar General Definition Last Amended 10/25/1993 A "cellar," except where a base plane is used to determine building height, is a space wholly or partly below curb level, with more than one-half its height (measured from floor to ceiling) below curb level. On through lots, the curb level nearest to such space shall be used to determine whether such space is a cellar. Where a base plane is used to determine building height, a cellar is a space wholly or partly below the base plane, with more than one-half its height (measured from floor to ceiling) below the base plane. In addition, the following rules shall apply: (a) When a sloping base plane is established, a cellar is a space wholly or partly below the street wall line level, with more than one-half its height (measured from floor to ceiling) below the street wall line level used to establish such base plane. > floor area General Definition Last Amended 12/5/2024 “Floor area” is the sum of the gross areas of the several floors of a building or buildings, measured from the exterior faces of exterior walls or from the center lines of walls separating two buildings. In particular, floor area includes: basement space, except as specifically excluded in this definition; elevator shafts or stairwells at each floor, except as specifically excluded in this definition; floor space in penthouses; attic space (whether or not a floor has been laid) providing structural headroom of eight feet or more; floor space in gallerias, interior balconies, mezzanines or bridges; floor space in open or roofed bridges, breeze ways or porches, if more than 50 percent of the perimeter of such bridge, breeze way or porch is enclosed, and provided that a parapet not higher than 3 feet, 8 inches, or a railing not less than 50 percent open and not higher than 4 feet, 6 inches, shall not constitute an enclosure; any other floor space used for dwelling purposes, no matter where located within a building, when not specifically excluded; floor space in accessory buildings, except for floor space used for accessory mechanical equipment; floor space used for accessory off-street loading berths in excess of 200 percent of the amount required by the applicable district regulations; floor space that is not otherwise exempt pursuant to this Section and is, or is made, inaccessible within a building; floor space in exterior balconies or in open or roofed terraces if more than 67 percent of the perimeter of such balcony or terrace is enclosed and provided that parapets, railings or safety guards, whether applied singly, or in combination, shall not constitute an enclosure when meeting the following criteria: parapets shall not exceed four feet in height; railings shall not exceed 4 feet, 6 inches, and shall be at least 50 percent open for the portion that exceeds four feet in height; and safety guards shall not exceed 10 feet in height and shall be at least 90 percent transparent for the portion that exceeds four feet in height. ### Permitext answer

No. On the stated facts, the 5,000-square-foot storage level is a cellar because more than one-half of its floor-to-ceiling height is below the applicable base plane. ZR 12-10 excludes cellar space from zoning floor area unless it is used for dwelling purposes. Because the level is not used for dwelling purposes, none of its 5,000 square feet counts as zoning floor area under the supplied definition.

This conclusion is limited to zoning floor-area treatment. The supplied text does not establish any separate use, occupancy, building-code, or other zoning requirement for the storage level.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Nondwelling cellar space is excluded from floor area: The floor-area definition generally counts gross floor areas but expressly excludes cellar space, except cellar space used for dwelling purposes. Since the stated cellar is used for storage and not dwelling purposes, its 5,000 square feet is excluded from zoning floor area.
